3. The following  paragr aphs describe the main findings on those sections of the  scorecard 
where the assessment was found to be lower than the maximum score for each criteri on (see 
details in annex) : 
(a) B.1.(i) Consistency be tween the Forest Refer ence Emissions  Level /Forest 
Reference Level  (FREL/F RL) and the green house gas ( GHG ) inventor y: the 
Assessment Team ( AT) noted that the information on GHG emissions provided in the 
GHG in ventory included in the latest B iennial Update  Report (BUR) (201 5) is not fully 
consistent with the information used  in the construction o f the FREL,  in particula r for 
the year 2011, and in terms of carbon pools and gases in cluded . The AT found that , while  
for the FREL P araguay used prelimi nary infor mation from the nat ional forest inventor y, 
for two forest strat a (the Bosque Subhúmedo del Ce rrado  and for the Bosque  Sub 
Húmedo Inundable del Río Paraguay  (BSHIRP) ) the GHG inven tory used the default 
values proposed by the Intergovernmental Panel o n Climat e Change ’s (IPCC) LULUC F 
Good Practi ce Guidance because  the data from the forest inventory w ere not availab le in 
time for the first Biennial Update Report ( BUR  (2015) . Furth ermor e, the GHG inventory 
takes into account CO 2 removals from one year´s growth after deforestation, wh ereas  
the FREL only  considers gross CO 2 emis sions from deforestation. A dditionally , the GHG 
inventory included carbon stock changes and nitrous oxide (N 2O) emissions from soils, 
but these were excluded from the FR EL because Parag uay deemed them not signifi cant 
(the funding proposal explains that the se emissions represent less  than 10  per cent  of 
the emissions in relation to CO 2eq). The AT consider ed that consi stency of the 
information provided a mong the documents should be an a rea of future im provement . 
In response to the se findings, Paraguay has proposed  (in its funding proposal ) an 
improvement plan  that will aim to address  them. The score , therefore , is 1 as there is 
evidence that inconsi sten cies will be resol ved, or jus tified,  in the next GHG inventory or 
FREL;  
(b) B.1.(vii) Accuracy of the FREL /FRL : some issues were raised duri ng the UNFCCC FREL 
Techn ical Assessment  (TA) that negatively affect the accuracy of Pa raguay ’s FREL 
rega rding the tr eatment of palm a reas a s an independent forest stratum for activity da ta 
and em ission factors, analysis of the deforested areas in line with  the forest definition, 
and the development of spec ific allometric equations for the e stimation of total and 
above -ground biomas s of tr ees by specie s within each identified forest st ratum. The AT 
acknowledged the intention expressed by Paraguay to continuo usly improve the 
collect ion of information on activity data, emission factors and omit ted carbo n po ols, as 
part of the stepwise approac h, for future FREL submissio ns (paragraph 44(b)  of the 
technical assessment report  (TAR )). Additionally, in its funding p roposal  Paraguay  
underli ned ongoing work and a proposed  improvement plan that will add ress the 
iden tified issues.  The sc ore is 1 : “significant issues were raised and we re resolve d; or 
issues that were not resolved due to the limitation of time and data but  wer e not 
material to th e accuracy of the FREL/FRL, and the country has pro vided a pla n on h ow it 
will seek to overcome them ”; 
(c) B.1.( ix) Inclusion of the most significant pools : the AT acknowledged  that Paraguay 
included in th e FREL th e most significant act ivity and the most signi ficant pools in terms 
of emissions from deforestati on (paragra ph 39 of the TAR) . Regarding the exclusion of 
soil organic carbon  in mineral soil s, Paraguay provided preliminary results of carbon 
content for each of the forest strata,  and information on the methodology that was used 
to derive them. The carbo n content e stimat ed for a soil de pth of 50 cm range s from 
32.35 to 64.07 t C/ha, with associated errors bei ng 18.08 per cent and 5.76 per cent. 
Paraguay explained that these prelimin ary results cannot be us ed for the estimation of 
CO 2 emissions from this p ool owing to the lack of informat ion on carbon content of soil 
for other land -use categories, which is need ed to assess the net variation of carbon


---
*Page 122*
---

 
GCF/B.2 4/02/Add. 07 
Page 121 
 
 
resulting from deforestation . The AT commend ed Paraguay fo r its efforts in the 
collection and provision of i nformation on thi s carbon  pool, a nd agree d that the lack of 
informat ion on carbon content of soil in other lan d uses prevents a proper estimation of 
the emissions, as the assumption of a full oxidation of t he carbon content of soil would 
overestimate the emissions fro m thi s pool . The AT t herefore considered that the 
omission of this p ool is, for the moment, adequa tely justified by Paraguay ; but, noting 
the ma gnitude of the preliminary  results on soil carbon stock provided by Paraguay, 
considered the assessm ent of the s ignif icance o f emis sions from this pool as an area of 
further improv ement . Likewise, the AT con sidered that, depending on the magnitude of 
the areas of organic soils in which deforestation takes  place, this pool might be a 
sign ificant source of  emissio ns a s there is a large quantity of carbon stored in these soils; 
therefore, the as sessment of the significance of emissions from this pool was  seen as an 
area for future impr ovement  (paragraph 33 of the TAR).  As regards the carbon pools 
dead wood a nd litte r, the AT considered  that they  are probably not significan t relative to 
other carbon pools, and therefore that their exclusion from the FREL  is justified ; 
however, the AT note d that Paraguay should enhance the information pr ovided to justify  
the omission of  these p ools  (paragraph 3 2 of the TAR ). In paragraph 44 ( b) of the TAR, 
the AT ackno wledged the interest of Para guay to c ontinuously improve the collect ion of 
information on activity data, emission factors and omitted carbo n pools . Moreover , the 
funding pr opos al notes that an a ction plan for future technical improvements has been 
launc hed that is assu med to address these issues. The score is 1 : “no, but justified due to

removals, noting that c ountri es should indicate a plan to include data in the future ”; 
(d) B.1.(x) Inclusion of all signif icant sources of emissions : the AT consider ed that the 
current  omission of pools and g ases is likely to be conservative in t he context of  the 
FREL. Nevertheless , the A T identified t he treatment of emissions of non -CO 2, and, 
specifically, emiss ions from the practice of slash and burn , as an area for future technical 
improv ement  (paragraph 43  (c) of the TAR ). Paraguay´s fundin g proposal  points out 
that the action plan for future technica l improvements is relevant for assessing and 
analy sing the significance of  emissions of gas es omitte d from the FREL.  The 
corresponding score is 1 : “no, but jus tified due  to lack of data and/or  the omissi on does 
not overes timate emissions, noting tha t coun tries shou ld indicate a plan to include data 
in the future ”; 
(e) B.1.(xi) Interg overnmental Panel on C limate Change  (IPCC ) guidance applied:  for 
the construction of  the natio nal FREL, Paraguay used th e metho dology provided in IPCC  
Good Practice Guidance for Land Use, Land -Use Change and For estry as a basis for  
estimating chan ges in carbon stock from the conversion of forest land to o ther land -use 
categori es. The 2006  IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Invent ories was a lso 
used for the selection of the default value of carbon fraction o f forest  biomass (see 
paragraph 11 of the TAR) . The score is therefore 1:  “guided by 200 3 good practic e 
guidelines”; 
(f) B.1.(x iv) Comparison with previous FREL/FRL : Para guay´s FREL is the fir st referen ce 
level submitted to the UNFCCC in the context of RBP . The corr esponding score  is 1: “no 
adjust ment made or n o previous FREL/FRL submi ssion ”; 
(g) B.1.(xv) Aggregat e FREL /FRL uncertainties : aggregate uncertainties estimates were 
not included in the F REL s ubmiss ion, as ackno wledged by Paraguay in its f unding 
proposal, section B.1.2 (xv) . The score is  therefore zero : “no informat ion on 
uncertainties provided or >50% ”; 
(h) B.1.( viii) Inclusion of all signi ficant activities : in paragr aph 35  of the TAR, the AT 
acknowledge d that the activity included by Paraguay in the FR EL ( reducing emissions

Monitoring System to supp ort the ongoing proc ess that spans each 12 -month peri od.

2.1 Impact potential                           Scale: High  
4. Paraguay is a landlock ed country with a population of 7 million people and an area of 
406,702 km2 from which in 2015 , according to the  World Bank, 55 per cent  was  agricultural 
land and ar ound 38  per cent  fore st land. Before 2015, the country had lost thousands  of


---
*Page 124*
---

 
GCF/B.2 4/02/Add. 07 
Page 123 
 
 
hect ares mainly to the expansion of the agricultural frontier for extensive  cattle ranging and 
soybean produ ction . Paraguay deforestation rate has been one of the largest worldwid e with an 
average los s of 286,000 hectares per year1.  
5. The Paraguay River divides t he fo rests into two distinct zones: those of the west , which  
are generally dry and poor; and those of the ea st which are more humid and inclu de the Gran 
Chaco regi on and the  Bosque Atlantico . The Gran Chaco which is shared by Argentina, Bolivia, 
Brazil  and Paraguay, is one of the most important defores tation hotspots worldwide . The 
Paraguayan Chaco lost  3,440,441 hectares from  2001  to 2014 (W orld Resources Institute ), 
largely  due to t he transfor mation of natural habitat s into crops and pastures.  The Bosque  
Atlantico  ecoregional compl ex, also present in Argentin a and  Brazil  and recognized as one of the 
world's biodi versity "hot spots" ,2 has also lost around  883 to 924 per cent  of its original cover .  
6. Land  tenu re has been historicall y driven by inequi table  land  assignments and 
acquisitions:  85 per cent  of the land in Paraguay is in the hands of 2.5  per c ent of the population 
(Oxfam). Only 6 .3 per cent  of the agricultural land is  in the h ands of small holder  peasants.  
7. Parag uay reduced emissio ns from deforest ation  at the national level i n the order of 
26,793,311.02 tCO 2eq for the period 2015 −2017 (23,0 20,721.90 t CO 2eq/year for 2015 –2016 
and 3,772,589.12 t CO 2eq/year for 2016 –2017) , through different effective policies a nd 
measures  that to ok place in th at period. The LULUCF  sector has historically  been the main 
source of GHG emissions in Paraguay.  
8. Paraguay completed its firs t BUR i n 2015, including the preparation of th e national GHG 
inventory for the LULUCF  sector , and later presented  BUR 2  in 2018. The Second Biennial 
Update Repo rt on Climate Change (BUR 2  −2018 ) quantifi ed LULUCF emissions at  a rate of 31  
per cent , behind those generat ed by the agricultural sector , estimated at 53  per cent . 
9. Para guay submitted its first FREL on a voluntary basis on 4  January 20 16, and  later a 
revised version taking into  consideration the clarifications and a dditional informatio n resulting 
from the technical assessment  to improve the transparency and consistency of the FREL. The 
development of Paraguay's FREL is based on historic al data  of forest cover changes for a 15 -
year period (2000 −2015), and covers the e ntire Paraguayan ter ritory.   
10. The  LULUCF experts concluded that the following areas for technical improvement 
identified in the report on the technical assessment of Paraguay’ s FREL also apply to the 
provision of information on the r esults of implementa tion of the activit y reducing emi ssions 
from deforestation: a) d evelopment of an improved system for collecting activity data  that 
enables the assessment of deforested areas in line wit h the adopted forest definition as well as 
the spat ially explicit identification o f palm areas; (b) c ontinuation of  work on the forest 
definition to ensure consistency across official submissions and in its practical implementation 
when assessing deforesta tion; (c) c onsideration of carbon removals from  land following 
deforestation to im prove consistency w ith the GHG in ventory and to avoid overestimation of 
emission reductions associated with the activity reducing emissions from deforestation; (d) 
collection  of information on the dynamics of carbon s tock s in the deadwood, litter and miner al 
soils pools afte r forest conve rsion in order to assess the significance, in terms of emissions, of 
these pools; (e) c ollection of the information needed to estimate emissi ons from organic soils

11. The methodologies provided for calculating emission  reductions are cle ar. Paraguay

12. The use of the pr oceeds ne eds better refinement to  ensure effective fut ure emission 
reductions from deforestation.  The proposal is to us e the proceeds to enable implementation of 
the Estrategia Nacional de Bosques para el Crecimiento Sostenible  (ENBCS ) (Nationa l Forest 
Strategy for Sustainable Gr owth), which is a guiding docu ment. The ENBCS includes a total of 41

actions). The proposal is to use around 58  per cent  of the proceeds to enable these actions with 
very broad definitions.  The proposal will u se around 42  per cent  of th e proceeds to define the

the country’s n ational ly determ ined contribution  and the ENBCS , and that specific eligibility 
criteria for each  of the sub windows  will be developed during th e first year of the project.   
13. RBPs provide Paraguay with a major opportunity in the fi ght against deforestation. 
However, RBPs alone cannot carry the whole financing weigh t nor can they  substitute for 
decisive political support and a high level of ambition . The climate chan ge fund  is simply  a 
mean s to achieve a result . As presented in th is ass essment , the Government of Paraguay could 
capitalize the fund and define incentives and disincentives  to ensure  positive impacts to  avoid  
deforestati on and to ensu re long-term sustainability  of the fund  with a real vision of change.  The 
impact will be measured not only based on the results of the past  but also on the effe ctive use of 
the pr oceeds , blending finance to stop defore station  and to  promot e regeneration of c ritical 
ecos ystems such as the Selva Atlantica and the Gran Chaco.  
2.2 Paradigm shif t potential     Scale: Mediu m-high 
14. Paraguay is a small country with important ecosy stems such as the Gran C haco and Mata 
Atlantica Fores ts. Unfortunat ely, du e to years of poli cies to exp and the agricultural frontier for 
cattle ranging and soy a production, the country allowed deforestation to take place.  From  2013, 
deforestation started to show  a decline , which was accentu ated in the 201 5−2017 period. 
However, the previous government  ame nded  Decree 7702 in September 2017, thus modifiyng 
the forestry law to enable deforest ation of forest reserves, with significant negative effects on 
land use change and  deforestat ion. Par aguay ’s current adm inistration abolished the Decree, 
closing  the windo w for furth er deforestation. Even though preliminary research show s that 
deforestation i s beginning  to decline , returning  to the levels of 2015 −2017, it is still early to 
claim positive results.  As shown  in the following table, the deforestation rates of 2017 returned  
to the level s of 2010 .

15. Changing the prac tices to stop  deforestation will  require strong commitments from the 
Government and the private sector, with  laws that remain  in place  independently of the change 
in government, and provide effectiv e deforestation m onitoring and controls.  
16. The proceeds will  be use d for implement ation of the ENBCS , includ ing a list o f actions 
intend ed to address direct and indirect causes of forest loss, barrier s to change and 
legal/regulatory framework s. 
17. The priority actions include, strengthening of the  Protected Areas Syst em (both public 
and private areas);  auditing and effective enforcement of the for estry and environmental legal 
framework, including the applic ation of penalties ; full implementation of Law 3001 on 
Payments for Environmental Services;  strengthening of the Environmental and Forestry 
Information System (monitoring syste m); support for imp lementing land -use planning; 
implementation of the Environmental and Social Man agement Framework;  strengthening of 
environmental and forestry management c apacities;  engagement  and development of 
sustainable economic alternatives for loc al actors  and knowl edge management and knowledge -
sharing .  
18. The proceeds  will also be u sed to cap itali ze the natio nal climate change fund for actions 
that will ensure carbon and non -carbon benefit s. However, the defi nition of the f und and 
innovatio n around a bene fit-sharing  mechan ism is still pending.  The proposal states that RBP 
from this fund  will give a signal to the G overnment that protecting the environment p ays off, 
implying a paradigm shift.  However, the GCF resources are limited , and shifting the  tendenc y to 
expand the agricultural frontier s will require  blending finance from public and private sectors 
with an innovative scheme that provides for comand and  control actions and incentives to 
promote  regerative a griculture . The Paragua yan economy will need to respond to the world 
markets that are shifting to wards  free deforestation  and renegerative value chains.  
19. The provision of innovative and targeted incentive s is a critical tool for forest 
conservation. The iTAP  note s that the proposal include s support to the fu ll implement ation of 
Law 3001/06 (Payments for Environmental Services). This is highly innovative, particularly 
because transactions happen  between private actors with defic its and sur pluses in the legally 
requ ired forest cover. The iTAP  welcomes this inve stme nt and would like to emphasize that the 
sustainability of the system of payments for environmental services is fully dependent on the 
enforcement of the fo restry law, which in turn requir es stro ng political support . 
20. The preparation and i mplementation of REDD-plus  in Paraguay involved consistent and 
broad participation processes, first under the U nited Nations -REDD National Programme during  
the period 2011 −2016,  and follow ing on from  this, through the Forest Carbon Partnership 
Facility  “Forests for Sustainable Growth” project from 2017  to the present.  The par ticipation 
process has been a key component in  development of the ENBCS inv olving projec t partners 
Institu to Forestal Nacional (National Fo restry Institut e, INFONA ), Federa ción para la 
Autodeterminac ión de los Pueblos Ind ígenas (Federation for the self -determination of 
indigenous communities, FAPI ) and Instituto Paraguayo del In digena  (Paraguayan Indigenous 
Institute, INDI ) and key actors in the publ ic sector, non- governme ntal organizations , civil 
society, a cademia and the private sector, with a focus on gender, and specific efforts to en sure 
effective part icipation from ind igenous peoples  and small holder  farme rs (campesinos ). The 
strategy was validated in 2018 through a process that  involved participation of 306 people , of

which 43  per cent  were women and 57  per cent  were men.  The REDD -plus  Working Group,

21. However, further consultations  should be undertaken to allow  participation and 
own ership of ENBCS activities.  Stake holder engageme nt should involv e private sector acto rs as

REDD -plus results.   
22. The project presents a gender assessment and  action plan, wh ich presents the si tuation 
of wom en in Paraguay a nd the six elements  that will be considered for gender mainstreaming in

gender equity u sing indicators and protocols for evaluation; (3)  establishing ove rsight through 
monitor ing, evaluation, auditing and reporting; (4) allocating enough hum an and financial

engag ement will be car ried out at the stage of siting, planning and designing actions ensuring

2.3 Sustainable developmen t potential         Scale: High  
23. The historic and contemporary rates of deforestation in Paraguay sugges t that 
environmental cons ervation is secondary t o an economic growth imperative based on 
agricultural expor ts. In order to shif t this t rend, there is a ne ed to showcase the im portance of 
standing forests for the provision of ecosystem ser vices at large. Pr eserving the forests in 
Paraguay, including the Gran Chaco a nd Selva Atlantica ecosystems , will contribute t o 
conserving a vast reposit ory of biological d iversity contributing  to the Aichi Biodiversity 
Targets.  
24. Paraguay depends on the export of energy from  its Itaipu hydroelectric  dam  that in turn 
depends on the stability of ecosystem services provided by forests. It is imperative for  Paraguay  
to understan d that there are large economic benefit s in stabi lizing the water cycle of the vast  
ecosystems that interact  to ma intain water cycles  in Latin American countries , including the 
Amazon rainforest, the Selva Atlantica and the Gran Chaco. According to Global Forest Watch 
the de partments of Alto Parana an d Canindeyú (where Itaipu is located) lost 237 ,900 hectares 
of forest between 2004 (when the zero deforestat ion law was first issued) and  2017. A W orld 
Resources Institute  study indicates that the annual cost f or Itaipu in terms of sediment ation 
from d eforestation actions costs US D 23 million annual ly. Forests are t herefore critical to 
ensure retention of sediments, and to allow long -term provision of energy from Itaipu.  
25. The benefits of standing forests are als o crucial to regula te rain and  water p rovision for 
consumption and agriculture . Ensuring water for agric ulture is only possible if the ecosystem 
services provided by fore sts are guaranteed. Other non -carbon be nefits in clude mitigation of 
soil erosion a nd s tabilization.   
26. In terms of social benefits, t he use of the proceeds should ensure more dir ect incentives 
or desincentives to the relevant stakeh olders  who drive deforest ation . Since 85  per cent  of the 
land in Paragua y is in the hands of 2.5  per cent  of the  population , the us e of the resources t hat 
will be deployed through the climate change f und  will need to be used wisely . Small amount s of 
payments for results might not change the bus iness as usual model of agri businesses. Rather, 
the use of the proceeds c ould be used be tter to analize the cu rrent financial and economic


---
*Page 128*
---

 
GCF/B.2 4/02/Add. 07 
Page 127 
 
 
models that are funding deforestation  On the other hand, the use of the proceeds shoul d also 
provide equitable and effici ent schemes for peasants and indige nous com munities th at live in 
only 6,3 per cent  of th e agricult ural land . They could use the RBP to pilot regeneration schemes 
with sustainable value chains that en sure better livelihoods.  
27. In addition, several efforts have been made  to suppo rt land titling, includ ing the w ork of 
the Nation al Institute for Ru ral and Land Development on a land cadaster and titling of land that 
followed the agrarian refo rm. This institute create d the Earth Resource Information System  to 
support t he land titling process . Complementary to this , the Pa raguayan In digenous Institute 
provided technical and legal assistance to indigenous communities to address claims and 
promote respect for t he lan d rights of these peoples . Land titling  will greatly  support the 
livelihoods of indigenous comm unities and small holders . 
28. Forest fires are als o increasing, mostly in the Western Region.  According to INFONA, 
about 60,000 hectares were  affected during A ugust and S eptember 2019. Other fire areas have 
been spotted in the Eastern Regio n of Par aguay but with less  severity. Forest fi res often have  a 
man-made origin s intended to expand the agricultural frontier. Therefore,  a strict er control and 
emergency respon se to the forest fires will benefit the effectiveness  of REDD -plus .  
2.4 Needs of the recipi ent                  Scale: Medium  
29. Econo mic growth  in Paragu ay depe nds on attracting foreign direct inve stment as well as 
on export ing electricity and  agricultural goods  such  as soybeans (Paraguay i s the fifth l argest 
soy p roducer in the world), beef and rice . Since 2014, Paraguay’s econ omy has grow n at a n 
average annual rate of 4 per cent due to strong production and high global pr ices (Central 
Intelligence Agency  World Factbook) . However, around a quar ter of Paraguayans live below the 
national poverty line. Much of the land is owned  by a small number of individual s and 
successive governments have been  slow to implement land reform ( British Broadcasting 
Corporation  country outlook). T he 2017 Gini index of Paraguay is  48 (Worl d Bank) , one of the 
highest in Latin America.  
30. Increased global demands for s oya and beef could impl y incre ased pressure on the 
forests. Breaking the  cycle will require  a national commitment, understand ing the need  for 
nature -based solutions to i ncre ase regen erative agriculture, avoid deforestation and ensure 
sustainable developmen t benefi ts.  
31. The countr y has mad e efforts to implemen t the forestry law and has taken actions to 
renew the z ero deforest ation  Law  in order  to control deforestation in the E astern Region. Action  
has also been taken  to streng then institutional capacity to co ntrol deforestat ion. In this context, 
payments are made  on the basis of proven results. However, the country has also seen t hat, 
following the change in article 42 of th e forestry l aw (by the previous government) , 
deforestation increased in just one year . Reducing  defores tation levels will imply more 
com mand and control options to ensure legal compliance  with the laws, better inform ation 
systems and strenghthening the capacity of the Ministry of Enviornment , INFO NA, the National 
Institute for Rural and Land  Dev elopment  and other relevant instituti ons; it will also imply 
giving  RBPs  to the end users, with in centives and d isincentives to stop de forestation . 
32. There is a special need to  drive more equitable sharing of the payments to indig enous 
communities and to  small holders  to ensure  better livelihoods.  Even though they live in a  small 
portion of the country and are not the mselves  driving deforestation , ther e could  be o ptions to 
allow equitab le land t itling and involve them in promoting  new renegerative econ omies. 
33. Paraguay is  dedicati ng just a small proportion of its budgetary expenditure to combat 
defores tation . Government spending in Paraguay averaged 2 ,457,896.80  million Paraguayan 
guaraní  (PYG ) (USD 387 ,662 ,389) from 1994 to  2019 (Trading E conomics, 201 9). The bulk of

budget ary exp enditures o n forest protection goes to  INFONA . Its budget is used  for

34. The use of the proceeds coul d add up to a bigger investment to  control deforestation an d 
incentivize rege nerative agriculture , for example using cross revenues from the electric sector 
to all ow for blended finance. The task of  support ing comma nd and control activities in the Gran 
Chaco and the Selva Atlantica ecosystems  will require  large investments, including on -the-
ground teams , vehicles and better information systems and data  among  other s.  
35. Inve stments in positive incentives are key t o main tain and further expand  low or zero  
deforestation rates  and to promote  rege neration . The proposal will use the proceeds to 
oper ationalize and overcome the current limitations of Law 3001/06 and enab le the market for

36. However, the Selva Atlantica and the Gran Chac o are  losing their effectiveness as 
standing ecosystem s due to the deforestation rates in  Argentina, Bolivia , Brazil  and Paraguay. 
Efforts to coor dinate REDD -plus  schemes in all countr ies are needed t o avoid further 
deterioration.   
2.5 Country ownership           Scale: High  
37. Paraguay has taken a ction s in REDD -plus activities under the forestry -related 
opportun ities created by  the U NFCCC and the Warsaw Framework for REDD- plus. The  
nationally  determ ined contribution of Paraguay play s an important role in policies at country 
level, with  the ambition to reduce deforestation by 20  per cent  based  on bus iness as usual 
projected for 2030.  
38. Paraguay has tak en a range of steps: 
(a) The Government of Para guay has developed  national l egislation to protect natural 
forests, prevent deforestation and restore fo rests in certain areas, with the Minist ry of 
Envi ronment and Sustainable Dev elopment (MADES ) responsib le for  general 
environmental supervision, and INFO NA in charge of applying the Fo rest Law 422/73. 
This is the main legal framework  for the forest sector in Paraguay, establishing 
incentives for reforestation; des ignating forests a s reserves  for forest productio n or as 
semi -protected forest; and establish ing regulations to protect fores t resources.  Through 
Law 422/73, farms with fores t areas are obliged to maintain 25  per cent  of the original 
forest area and forest type. In additi on, forests must be protected near water courses 
(with a buffer zone of 100 me tres on each side)  and where re storation of s uch forest 
takes place, this  must be carried out wit h native species;  
(b) The National Commission on Cl imate Change was consolidate d through the Cl imate 
Change Law. The Commission is composed of permanent members and advisory 
mem bers , including  representatives of public instit utions, private sector, non-

govern mental organizations  and indigenous organizations, among others. This national 
entity deals with issue s related to cli mate c hange, including REDD -plus; 
(c) In 2011 th e country starte d its REDD -plus readiness phas e to develop and strengthen 
national capa cities for REDD -plus  through official development assistance and  national 
initiatives;   
(d) The Land Co nversion Moratorium for the Atlantic Forest of Paraguay, also know n as  the 
“Zero Defor estation Law” , was enacted in 2004 and dramatic ally slowed the c ountry’s 
deforestation rate by prohibiting the t ransformation and conversion of forested areas in 
Paraguay 's Eastern Region . This  law was extended until 2020 ;  
(e) The Law 3001/ 06 on Valuation and  Compensation of En vironmental Services is 
cons idered a key mea sure to implement REDD -plus  in the country, as it co nsiders the 
benefits provided to the population di rectly or indirectly by manageme nt activities, 
conservation and restoration  of ecosystem fu nctions ; 
(f) In 2014, Parag uay approved its National Developme nt Plan after a participatory process

(g) In 20 18, Paraguay pre sented its ENBCS  following ex tensiv e stakeholder consultations

39. However, assurance to maintai n the forestry law depends  on the  willingn ess of each 
new government . Within the f orestry law, Paraguay issued  Decree No. 7 702/17, adopted in 
September 2017 by the previous government administration , that  modified article 42 of the 
forestry law and c reated a legal framework that en abled the defore station of forest reserv es. 
This decree had a significant negative effect on land -use change in Paraguay.  Paraguay's current 
administration abolished Decree No. 770 2/17, closing the window of opportunity crea ted for 
deforestation, which had  openly contradi cted the environmental g oals of both MADES and 
INFONA.   
40. Even  though the current go vernment reversed the Decree and has the willingness to 
support REDD -plus  schemes,  there is a need to see this effort reflected in  a more ambitious 
financial scheme , forward -thinking policies a nd public -priv ate coalitions that are able to ensur e 
more permanent and s calable schemes.  
41. The funding proposal has be en developed in close co llaboration with MADES and 
INFONA and in close consultatio n with the country’s n ational designat ed authority and the 
planning  secretariat ( Secretaria Tecnica de Planificación del Desarrollo Económico y Social ). 
Developmen t of the  ENBCS has been carried out  within a broader consultation process.  
42. A steering commi ttee for the RBPs accou nt will be estab lished an d will be responsible 
for the approval of disbursements. The committee will invol ve nine  members, in cluding the 
Minister of Envi ronment and Sustainable Development  MADES , the Minister of A griculture  
MAG , the Nat ional Forestry Ins titute  INFONA , the Minister of Economic and Social Development , 
one r epresentative of i ndigenous peoples, an non- governmental organization  representative, a 
representative of pr ivate sector associations and a representati ve of academia. Fou r alternates 
will also be  appointe d who will temporarily replac e official  members in cas e of illness or 
absence or o ther temporary impairment. This will ensure  effective governance if th e non-
governmental representatives are ab le to unite their constit uencies  to effectively  implement  
REDD -plus incentives.  
2.6 Efficienc y and effec tiveness      Scale: Medium

43. Paraguay h as presented the payment fo r results  repor t, accounting for 76  per cent  of the 
total volu me of REDD -plus results bet ween 201 5 and 201 7 (measu red against FREL B  – the 
period of 2011−2015 ), equivalent to a total of 23,000,000 .0 tCO 2eq . This i ncludes a buffer of 18  
per cent . The evaluation of the s corecard has reduced total scores from 4 8 to 36 and therefore  
emission reductions  were  reduc ed to 1 4,145 ,000  tCO 2eq, includi ng 2.5 per cen t of non-carbon 
benefits. This amount  is equivalent  to USD  72,493 ,125  at USD  5 per tCO 2eq. However, as the 
United Nations  Environment Programme ´s accreditation  only al lows them  to manage projects  
for a maximun of USD 50 million , the curr ent p roposal is for  that maximun amount. A 
subsequent proposal will be submitted to account for the  remainin g USD 22,493 ,125 . 
44. The effecti veness of the u se of proceeds can only be evaluated in the future, when the 
impact of the measures implemen ted through the national strate gy for the sustaina ble growth 
of fore sts (ENBCS) and the scheme to provide incentives and d isincentve s through the climate 
change fund produce expected results in terms of chang ing behavior s and avoid ed 
deforestation .  
45. Paraguay  proposes to us e around 58 per cent of the resources to strenghthen  
institutional capacities  and improv e the enabling enviro nment and the monitoring syste m to 
implement ENB CS, and  42 per cent  of the proceeds to capitali ze the climate change fund. The 
current bud get propo ses an importa nt investment in monito ring and s trengthen ing of 
operational capacities at INFONA and MADES, including proper enforcement of the forestry law. 
Thes e are welcome i nvestments. However, they should go hand  in hand with unequivocal 
political supp ort for both INFONA and MADES in ca rrying out  their moni toring and enforcement 
duties. Otherwise, the investment will not be effective.  
46. The Ministry of Environme nt and Sustaina ble Development together with INFO NA could 
use the Paraguayan Government co -fund ing to ensure these institutions a re strengthened  
(consultancies, staffing etc)  and use the proceeds to en sure more permanent  outcomes to avoid 
deforestation over the l ong run.  
47. The USD 18.66 million assign ed to the climat e change fund is small amount  to start an 
effect ive mechanism to incentivize good pr actices  and disincen tivize agri businesses that are 
driving defor estation. The implementation of Law  3001/06 that established a system of 
payment s for ecosystem se rvices will need a constan t flow of resource s to ensure pr oper 
sustainability. There is a need to dev elop a much more ambitious financial  mechanism that 
blends finance from the private sector and public resources , ensuri ng susta inab ility of the fund . 
For example, using  resources from the energy s ector for the climate change fund on a more 
sustainable and perma nent basis.   
48. Following on the subject of incentives against d eforestation and the full implementation 
of law 3001/06, the iTAP  notes that the G over nment of Paraguay has the obligation  to purchas e 
environmenta l certificates for projects considered as of high e nvironmental impact. In designing 
the climate change fund, t he Government of Paraguay c ould pre pare  and di sclose  a report o n 
compliance statu s and pursue ways forward  that are  aligned with the REDD -plus  strategy.  
49. Paraguay has establish ed a round table on sustainable finances to bridge the financial 
sector to the value chains  that are dri ving deforestation. This is an ideal platform to develop 
financial s chem es that can effectively influence behaviour of  the agri businesses.  
50. There is also an u rgent need to start “defunding deforestation ”. In other words , 
Paraguay will nee d to develop a deep analy sis of the subsidies given to land ow ners and all the 
incentives and d isincent ives in ter ms of land acqu isition, l and refor m, fiscal regimes and 
financial aspects  that are supporting agri businesses witho ut consider ing the cons equences of 
deforestation.  
51.  As the world mov es to prevent  deforestat ion and encourage renegerative supply c hains , 
the soya and beef farmers of P araguay  will need  to revise th eir mo dels , and the countr y will

need to rev ise trade agreements , shifting to new methods  in order to compete in the world 
markets.  
52. As stated a bove, Paraguay has grown  steadily in the last 1 0 years. Th ere coul d be an 
increase in government expenditure to control  deforestation and to support regeneration 
models. The current budget given to the Ministry of Enviroment  and Sustainable Develop ment  
and to INFO NA seem insuffi cien t to ensure an effective REDD -plus strategy. 
III. Overa ll rem arks from the independent Technical Advi sory Panel  
53. The Paraguay REDD- plus project proposal presents a good case for financ ing under the 
GCF REDD -plus pilot program me. However, because of its nature, the p roject proposal lacks 
sufficient information on the efficacy of the utilization of proceeds for the iTAP  to make specific 
and decisive remarks.  
54. The iTAP  recommends this p roject be approved  by the Board.  
55. The iTAP  recommends that Paraguay and the accre dited entity give  speci al attention to 
the following:  
(a) Cont inue efforts to improve the FREL , prioritizing:  
(i) Implementation of the proposed improvement plan in order to (a) addre ss the 
incon sistencies between the FREL and the GHG inventory; (b) improve  the 
collection of i nform ation on a ctivity d ata, emission factors  and om itted carbon 
pools ; and (c) assess the significance of emissions of gases omitted from the 
FREL;  
(ii) Estimate and  report the ag gregate FREL and uncertainties related to res ults in 
future submissi ons; 
(iii) Fina lize the studies  allowing for t he defini tion of f orest d egradation  as well as of 
methods and processes for generating activity data and emission factors, s o that 
it may be possible to analys e the significance of the emissions fr om this activity ; 
(iv) Continue capacity -building efforts  to improve  accuracy of t he data and 
information included in the technical annex, particularly regarding the 
considerati on of post -deforestation carbon stock s and the methods to estimate 
activit y data ; and 
(v) Finalize the e stablishment of a syste m allowing regist ration and track ing o f 
emiss ion reductions from REDD -plus  activities; and  
(b) Given the accreditation scope of the accredited  entity  with a ceiling of USD 50 million , 
the addition al RBPs tha t the h ost country is entitled to  might  be addressed in a futu re 
funding proposal for a sub seque nt Board m eeting.  
56. In terms of the use of the proceeds for the potential subsequent proposal, the long -term 
sustainability of the n ational climat e change fund is fundamen tal to build on this funding 
proposal . Paraguay proposed several lin es of action  towards this goal, including contributions 
from the public treasury , private sources and others. The iTAP  would like to see thes e lines of 
action  confirme d with a  plan around scalability , efficiency, capitalization and inno vative 
blended financ e, incentive  and disincentive  mechanisms , if Paraguay  returns to GCF for 
additional RBPs.  
57. Furthermore, the iTAP  would welco me mechanisms and provision s to drive actions for 
“defunding def orestation” and to involve agri businesses in def orestation -free commo dity value 
chains t hat respond to the urgent nee d to stop deforestation and ensure regenerative 
agricultural production in  Paraguay.

involving indigenous c omm unities and peasan ts (campesin os).

 1 Some issues were raised during the TA that negatively affect 
the accuracy of Paraguay´s  FREL : 
• The AT  noted that while the national forest invent ory 
consider s palm areas  as an independent forest 
stratum, for which dasometric information is 
available, th e original F REL submission did not

is situated. F urthermore, Paraguay stated that the 
available information, collected over three sample 
units, i s considered not reliable enough because it was

collected i n a small area that does not fully represent

by area of the emission facto rs est imated 
independently for these two forest strata, whic h was 
multip lied by deforested areas estimated for “Bosque 
Sub Húmedo Inundable del Río Paraguay” t o derive 
the C O2 emissions from deforestation in this forest

but considered  the treatment of palm areas  as an 
indep endent forest stratum, in terms of activity data 
and emission factors, would e nhance th e accuracy of 
the estimates, avoiding the potential overestimation 
of emissions, and therefore this is an area for future 
technical improvements . (paragraph 23 of th e TA R). 
• During the TA week, Parag uay acknowledged that, 
owing to the medium- resolution sate llite images u sed 
and technical constraints during the photo-
interpretation of the images, in practice only areas

per c ent) can be unequivocally identified, and

when elaborating the 2011 forest cover map and the

definition is an area for future improv ement  
(paragraph  22 of th e TAR) . 
• The AT noted that the  allometric eq uations used to

the level of each forest str atum, therefore leading to a 
potential  overestimation or underestimation of the

of data needed for estimati ng GHG emissions  (paragraph 3 0 of

As regar ds the pool soil organic carbon in mineral soils, 
Paraguay provided preliminary result s of carb on c ontent for 
each of the forest strata, and in formation on  the methodology 
that was used to derive them.  Paraguay explained that these 
preliminar y results cann ot be used for the estimation of CO2 
emissions from this pool owing to the lack of inf ormation on 
carbon content of soil for other land- use cat egories, whi ch is 
needed to assess the net variation of carbo n resulting from 
deforestation. Furthe rmore, Paraguay clarified in the modified 
submission that CO2 emissions from this pool, when forests  
are conv erte d to cropland and other lands, as included i n its 
GHG in ventory, were estimated assuming that all the car bon in 
soil is oxidized after the conv ersion. The AT  agreed  that the 
lack of information on carbon content of soil in other land uses 
prev ents a pr oper  estimation of the emissions, as the 
assumpt ion of a ful l oxidation of the carbon content of soil 
would o vere stimate the emissions from this po ol. The AT 
therefore considered  that the omission of this pool is, for the 
moment, adequately justif ied by Pa ragu ay; but, noting the 
magnitude of the prelimi nary results  on soil carbon stock 
provided by Paraguay, consi dered the assessment of the 
significan ce of emission s from this pool as an area of further 
improvement (paragraph 3 1 of the TAR). . 
Paraguay explain ed in the modified submission that soil 
organic carbon in or ganic soils was excluded from the 
proposed FRE L owing to the lack of scientific knowledge  and 
data on  carbon content from field measurements, and stated 
during the exchange of information  that some st udies could be 
launched using available reso urces to ass ess the total areas of 
these soils within the cou ntry. The AT considered  that, 
dependin g on the magni tude of the areas of organic soils in 
which deforestation takes place, this pool might be a 
significa nt source of emissions as there is a large quantity of  
carbon stored in these soils; therefore, the a ssessment of the 
significance of emissions  from this p ool was  seen as an area 
for future improvement  (paragraph 33 of the TAR).

 Response  to the i ndependent Technical Advisory Panel's  assessment 
(FP 121) 
Proposal name:  REDD+ Results -based payments in Paraguay for the period 2015 -2017  
Accredited entity:  United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)  
Impact potential  
Paraguay takes note of the comments and recommendations. The country is currently 
refining the structure of the National Climate Change Fund (NCCF). The actions selected from 
the ENBCS, which are detailed in Table 14 of the proposal, comprise m ajor investments in 
enforcement  and monitoring  capacities, investments in response/alert  capacities, 
including fire response, and investment s in incentives  (Law 3001/06; income 
diversification). Paraguay and the AE are already working on strengthening the financial sustainability of the NCCF . Finally, based on 2017 data  reported in the BUR , the area of 
remaining forests in Paraguay is 4 2%. This includes larg e tracks of continuous forests.  
Paradigm shift potential  
Paraguay fully agrees on that success in the implementation of Law 3001 requires 
enforcement of the forestry law, which in turn requires strong political support. The proceeds from results -based payments (RBPs) will be used to improve enforcement capacities and 
political support . Paraguay agrees on the need to continue consultations to maintain and 
increase ownership of the ENBCS. Participation has been a highlight of the REDD+ proce ss 
and the government is committed to keep it that way. We also confirm that as stated in the project document  there will be a sustained engagement on gender issues during 
implementation of this proposal.  
Sustainable development potential  
Paraguay take s note of comments and recommendations. A key strategy is to increase 
capacities to enforce  the forestry law in medium and large farms while in parallel 
strengthening incentives  to protect forest cover (Law 3001/06). The proposal also includes, 
as recommen ded by the i TAP, income diversification  schemes to improve livelihoods for 
small landholders and communities. We also agree with the importance of understanding the 
current financial and economic models that result in deforestation. This is work in progress in 
Paraguay, to which RBPs will also contribute. Finally, the proposal includes a major 
investment in fire response capacities , as the i TAP recommends.   
Needs of the recipient  
Paraguay takes note of the suggestions from  the iTAP to apply innovative financial 
mechanisms to increase investments to combat deforestation, including contributions from 
the energy sector and blended finance. The NCCF includes a window intended to tap private 
sector investment . We agree with facilitating indigenous communities and small peasants 
access to RBPs. Paraguay also acknowledges the need to increase its own investments into 
deforestation activi ties. Finally, while the country has been growing in the past years, unmet


---
*Page 152*
---

  
       GCF/B.2 4/02/Add.07  
Page 151 
 
 
 
 basic needs are still high. Improving human development will require substantial and steady 
public investment in social areas.  
Country ownership   
Paraguay acknowledges the recommendations from the i TAP. There has been increasing 
ownership of the REDD+ p rocess by stakeholders and RBPs will help to keep and increase 
participation . We take note that the current administration reversed the Decree that had 
allowed a window for deforestation in the country  (the strong negative reaction from the 
public  was important in driving this decision ). We also acknowledge that it will be necessary  
to complement this effort with ambitious financial scheme s, forward -thinking policies and 
public -private coalitions for anti -deforestation actions . The proposed use of RBPs, 
particularly through the NCCF, include actions specifically targeted towards thes e objectives.  
Efficiency and effectiveness   
Paraguay would like to assure the iTAP that RBPs will not be used to pay for MADES and/or 
INFONA´s staff, nor they will support unnecessary studies and consultancy. These will be kept 
to the bare minimum in order to maximize investments in capacities for enforcement , 
monitoring  and the stre ngthening of incentives  for forest conservation. Paraguay also take 
due note of the need to increase budgetary allocations to control deforestation and support 
forest restoration. Paraguay commits to ensure a budget to cover operational/recurrent costs 
for INFONA and MADES to complement RBPs and make an efficient use of them.  
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:   
 
Paraguay would like to thank the i TAP for the constructive review. B oth Paraguay and the AE 
commit to undertake the necessary actions to address the comments and recommendations  
as set in the Overall Remarks . In doing so, we would like to highlight that the description of 
eligibility and areas of priority of the NCCF, as it shows in Annex 8, provides assur ances  to 
the GCF and the donor community that RBPs will be invested efficiently  and will comply 
with the GCF and AE policies . The NCCF also includes a dedicated window to leverage 
private sector resources  under a variety of investment models. MADES and INF ONA will 
continue to work with ongoing local initiatives to involve agribusiness in deforestation -free 
commodity value chains. Finally, the recommendations aimed to improve accuracy of the 
FRELs and the reporting of results are genuinely welcome. Paraguay has already started to 
work on some of them and plans to address them all in the short and medium term.

1. Introduction  
 
Understanding the specific situation of women  and men  is an important prerequisite  for addressing gender 
inequality, as well as for generating appropriate and comprehensive responses. It is essen tial to increase 
the availability of  gender -disaggregated data and analysis of gender -related  information to guide the design 
of programmes.  Data collection and analysis are fundamental inputs to  allow us to identify where the gaps 
between the situation o f women and men are, describe their characteristics , measure their magnitude and 
identify the specific needs of women  and men . 
 
In the development of the current proposal “ Paraguay REDD+ RBP for results period 2015 -2017 ”, UN 
Environment assess ed potential roles, benefits, impacts and risks for women and men . The results of this 
assessment and resulting actions are summariz ed in the present document. The gender assessment will 
be further refined a t the beginning of the project and throughout its implementati on. Based on the 
assessment , the project will include actions  to avoid, minimize, and/or mitigate any adverse gender impacts 
or risks, and at the same time implement positive actions to contribute to gender equality and empowerment 
of women  in the forestry  and land -use sector. An initial action plan setting out what is to be done and by 
whom, and providing indicators, targets and a budget, has been drawn up and will be updated at key points 
throughout the life time of the project ( i.e. inception stage, annu al reviews, mid -term evaluation).  
 
Particular attention is paid to the different challenges faced by women and girls within indigenous 
communities and other minority groups,  who often face a double discrimination , and to promot ing the 
participation and lea dership of women in all activities. There are six elements t hat will be considered for 
gender mainstreaming in the design and implementation of the activities of this proposal; these are:  
 
i) strengthening accountability for gender equity at different levels ;  
ii) enhancing results -based management for gender equity usin g indicators and protocols for 
evaluation;  
iii) establishing oversight through monitoring, evaluation, auditing and reporting;  
iv) allocating enough  human and financial resources;  
v) capacity building for gender mainstreaming; and 
vi) ensuring  coherence, knowledge and information management.  
 
The main needs and priorities of women and men that were identified through stakeholder engagement 
activities undertaken in the preparation of the project include those r elated to : access to natural resou rces, 
including for food, medicine, sustainable commercial and other livelihood -related activities; formalization of 
land tenure; and equal access to economic opportunities and benefit sharing.  
 
A gender specialist will be  hired for the Project Management Unit of the programme  and provided with a 
comprehensive mandate , which will ensure gender mainstreaming throughout the design and 
implementation of project activities . UN Environment will undertake further gender assessmen ts at the 
activity level and  update and expand the action plan at the project level according to identified needs and 
to UN Environment and GCF guidance on gender.  
2. Existing gender dynamics and inequalities in Paraguay  
 
In Paraguay, women and men experi ence different socio -economic conditions. The less favo urable 
situation of women is related to socio -cultural factors, linked to  the division of labour, which affect the


---
*Page 156*
---

Gender Assessment and Action Plan  
Document associated with the Pro posal for REDD+ Results -Based Payments  
in Paraguay for the Period 2015 -2017   
 
 
4 
 possibility of access to education, training and technical advice, credit  and land own ership. Lower education 
levels and limited access to specific training and information in turn limit prospects of economic 
empowerment and possibilities  to generate income and make decisions about expenditures and resources. 
Indigenous women face even grea ter discrimination. Within their own communities, they have less decision -
making power than men over land use and less access to land.  Cultural barriers  have an enormous impact 
and are often  difficult to overcome. Customs, traditions, beliefs  and stereotyp es still hamper the exercise 
of rights that have been explicitly recognized in the legal framework  of the country . 
Evidence of these unequal circumstances is provided by  the statistics presented below.  
a. Gender Equality  Index  
 
The Gender Equality Index is a  tool that measures how far or close countries are from achieving a gender -
equal society, where 100 stands for the best possible situation.  The Gender Equality Index measures 
gender inequalities in three important aspects of human development: health, empo werment and economic 
status.  
 
 
b. Education  
 
With respect to  gender gaps in education in Paraguay, the country has made progress in recent years, 
nevertheless there is still lim ited access of girls and women to higher education, especially in rural areas.  
 
 
 
c. Labo ur force participation rate and unemployme nt rate  
 
Women in Paraguay, particularly in rural areas, have  lower economic revenues than men.  Women’s access 
to the labour force is limited and strongly determined by social and family  circumstances.  Due to domestic 
and care responsibilities, women in both rural and urban areas often have greater restrictions than men in 
participating in  the formal labour force , and in terms of receiving regular salaries, social security, work 
stability and union protection.  Economic growth in Paraguay has contributed little to wards  reducing  the 
economic gender gap.

d. Political participation rate  
 
Women’s participation in politics and decision -making processes in Paraguay have improved  in recent 
years , but still reflect significant inequalities. There has been a slow increase in the number of women in 
elected  positions. The Electoral Code establishe s quotas for women, but these are only valid for internal 
selection  of candidates  in parties  and therefore the quotas do not necessarily have  an impact on general 
election results. In the high -level political  positions , women  have a low representation. This reflects the 
difficulties that women still have to actively participate in decision -making.  
 
Proportion of seats held by women in 
national parliaments and local 
governments in the public sector  81.40% held by Men and 18.60% by 
Wom en (2016)7 
 
e. Health and sexual self-determination  
 
Maternal mortality is among the highest in the region, and Paraguay also has high rates of teenage 
pregnancy. The physical autonomy of women is affected by violence both inside and  outside of the family, 
as well as by  limited choice in terms of sexuality and reproduction.  
 
Maternal mortality rate  132 / 100,000 children born alive (2015)8

f. Access to land  
 
In Paraguay, access to land is still a factor that determines the livelihoods  of most of  the rural populati on. 
Current land tenure is characterized by a huge concentration of land in the hands of a small group of 
landowners. 0.4% of the landowners  manage m ore land than the remaining 99.6%.  
Women suffer  even more from land tenure inequality than men . Between 19 40 and 2008, women received 
13.6% of fiscal land allocated to peasant families. They have also been marginalized in access to productive 
inputs and land-related services.12 
According to a UN Women publication on land and gender in Paraguay13, “increased cont rol of land by 
women could contribute to the survival of peasant and indigenous communities in the country, to 
sustainable local development, to food sovereignty, to the conservation of biodiversity and forests, while 
also contributing to the mitigation an d adaptation of climate change.  It is not only necessary to transform 
the current land tenure structure, but also to invest in improving the living conditions of the rural population 
and, above all, to accompany the distribution of land with p olicies takin g into account the needs and 
differentiated impact on men and women in order to achieve real equality. ”  
There is widespread consensus on the need to eliminate the gender inequality reflected in the figures 
above, and to empower women with acc ess to inform ation, training, tenure and other resources. To this 
end, the project aims to ensure a comprehensive approach that fully and equally incorporates women ’s 
perspectives and contributions  (see section s 4, 5 and 6  below) .

gender equality

Paraguay is signatory of several international conventions that focus on gender equality and women's

 • The Convention on the Eliminat ion of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW),  
adopted in 1979 by the UN General Assembly, is often described as an international bill of rights 
for women. Consisting of a preamble and 30 articles, it defines what const itutes discrimination 
against women and sets up an agenda for national action to end such discrimination.15 The 
convention was ratified by Paraguay in 1987.

b. National frameworks, policies and laws  
 
There is recognition  in the policy and legal framework of Paraguay of the  above -mentioned realities, and 
efforts have been made to promote gender equality through, for example, the following frameworks, policies 
and laws.  
The National Constitution of 1992  guarantees equality of rights and non -discrimination, explicitly 
establis hing equality of persons, equality of rights between women and men and the requirement of non-
discrimination, providing that the State must promote the conditions and create adequate  mechanisms for 
such equality to be real and effective, removing obstacles  that impede or hinder its exercise and facilitating 
the participation of women in all spheres of national life.  
• Art. 46: Equality of people  
• Art. 47: Guarantees of equality  
• Art. 48: Equal rights of men and women  
• Art. 60: Protection against violence  
• Art. 88 : Non -discrimination  
• Art. 89: Women's work  
Furthermore, Paraguay created a Women's Secretariat in 1992, which became the Women's Ministry  in 
2012  (Law 4675/12 elevates the Secretariat of Women to the Ministry of Wom en).  It also developed a 
National Plan for Equal Opportunities between Women and Men for the period 2008 -201716, in 
addition to a National Gender Strategy on Climate Change , published in 2017.  
Additionally, Paraguay also relies on the following l egal instruments tha t consider gender aspects:  
• Law 5777/16 on comprehensive protection for women against all forms of violence.  
• Law 5808/15 on promotion  and protection of motherhood and support for breastfeeding.  
• Law 5446/15 on Public Policies for Rural Women , which  has as its general objective to promote 
and guarantee the economic, social, political and cultural rights of rural women  that are  fundamental 
for their empowerment and development.   
In November 2018, the National Government, through the Ministry of Women, organi zed the first of a series 
of planned working meeting s among the units in charge of gender issues across the Executive Authority ,

3. Gender, forests and REDD+ in Paraguay  
 
According to FAO, approximately 1.2 billion people around the world depend on agroforestry and forest 
resources for their livelihoods. At least 50 %of these are women, which corresponds to a total of around  600 
million women liv ing in and depend ing on fores ts. However, the forest sector generally still lacks gender 
equality considerations , affecting women´s active participation in forest governance. W hen looking at the 
management of forests , we need to be aware that men and women often have different roles a nd needs 
and a different relationship to the forest and forest -related resources. So far, those differences were often 
unknown or ignored by interv entions related to forest management, which led either to failed initiatives or 
to increases in gender inequa lities within the communities. The gender approach in forestry starts with a 
better understanding of the role women play in the management of fores t resources18. 
Economic, social, cultural, political and legal environments affect the right and ability of wo men and men to 
control forest resources and own land. Access to land alone does not guarantee access to forest products 
and opportunities to generate forest -related income s. 
In many countries , women are the primary users of certain forest resources and the refore they play an 
essential role in forest management, conservation and forest -based climate change mitigation and 
adaptat ion. As they heavily depend on forests and forests products , there is more at stake for women when 
forest s are degrade d or disappear . 
In Paraguay, t here are more challenges to address inequality gaps between men and women in relation to 
economic opportunit ies, health and education in rural areas than in urban areas.   
In areas with high forest cover and high poverty rates, there tends t o be a relatively high dependence of 
communities on forests for their livelihoods, especially in adverse economic situations . Implementation of 
REDD+ actions designed with the participation of local stakeholders and communities, and with a gender 
approach,  can benefit local livelihoods by helping to clarify and strengthen land tenure rights, increasing 
community capacity for fo rest management, and by maintaining ecosystem services important for both food 
security and climate change adaptation.  
The design an d implementation of REDD+ policies and measures in Paraguay are based on a gender 
mainstreaming approach, in accordance with a wide range of national laws and commitments  (see above) , 
and specific objectives of different projects and programmes, including the UN -REDD Paraguay National 
Programme.  
In the final evaluation19 of the UN -REDD National Programme in Parag uay, challenges related to gender, 
and vulnerabilities of women, were highlighted, including the fact that in Paraguay, 1% of the population 
owns 8 0% of the land, and women are owners of just 27% of private land  (see also section 2f above) .  
There was also  an observation that c limate finance, when merged with private sector investment, tends to

highligh ted as elements to be recognized and promoted when designing and implementing climate policy 
and incentives.  
a. REDD+ Gender Approach  
 
The UN -REDD Programme's gender approach pursues three complementary objectives, as follows:  
• Ensure that the principles of g ender equality and the empowerment of women inform the 
formulation, prioritization and implementation of REDD+ policies and measures and are fully 
integrated into them;  
• Comply with the provisions on gender equality contained in international agreements on REDD+, 
including safeguards; and  
• Promote gender mainstreaming in planning activities, i mplementation, monitoring and knowledge 
management of REDD+ in order to apply REDD+ measures with a gender perspective and 
contribute to SDG 5 on gender equality.  
The five pillars of  the UN-REDD Programme´s gender methodology are as follows (a detailed des cription 
can be found in the Informe Metodológico de ONU -REDD sobre Género , 2017) : 
I. Gender -sensitive evaluations and gender -specific analyses  
II. Gender awareness and capacity -building  
III. Participation with a gender perspective  
IV. Planning  and monitoring with a gender perspective  
V. Gender knowledge management  
These orientations have guided much of the work on embedding gender considerations within Paraguay’s 
activities on REDD+ in recent years and  will continue to be relevant as the country begins to scale up 
implementation.  
b. Gender considerations in the “Estrategia Nacional de Bosques par a el Crecimiento 
Sostenible ” (ENBCS)  
 
The ENBCS has been constructed collectively, with a gender approach and the promotion of participation 
of key stakehold ers, including women’s groups and civil society. These key actors were identified and 
involved in the REDD+ work following the guidelines  of the stakeholder identification and mapping  carried 
out in 2015 within the framework of the UN -REDD National Progr amme and the " Stakeholder Participation 
Plan" developed for the ENBCS. The process of development, socialization and validation of the ENBCS, 
and the policies and measures it establishes, was carried out with representatives of the public sector, 
private sector, c ivil society, academia, indigenous peoples, small farmer ( campesino ) organizations, and 
financial entities, with a cross -cutting gender a pproach and continued engagement and participation of 
women’s and civil society organizations.  This participation of k ey actors has been formalized through the 
REDD+ Working Group, which was created and formed within the framework of the National Commissi on 
on Climate Change, with the aim of becoming a space for discussion and technical consultations on issues 
related to REDD+.

10 The ENBCS has six strategic and three  cross -cutting measures, under which several actions include 
specific gender approaches and  other actions focus on vulnerable populations  in general, including women . 
The ENBCS aims to reduce gender gaps spec ifically by:  
•Ensuring the participation of women in all spheres of decision making related to forests, considering
the diffe rentiated roles related to forests and dependency over forest  resources between men and
women;
•And by aiming to ensure equal access to services like forest related education, credit and also to
land.
Specific actions  in the ENBCS which can help to address vulnerability in general include: 
•Diversify income sources and sustainable production systems, including through the use of mult iple
benefits from forests;
•Facilitate access for local communities and women's groups to formal cre dit systems for the
adoption of better production technologies that can contribute to emissions reductions;
•Strengthen the market for differentiated product s from forests;
•Promote energy efficiency programs in local and indigenous communities, focused on t he domestic
use of firewood from the native forest, and highlighting the role of women;
•Develop and incentivize production processes through the implementat ion of agroforestry,
silvopastoral, and forest management models; and
•Prioritize payment for environmental services in indigenous communities.
To achieve gender equality, the ENBCS seeks to reduce the economic gap between men and women in 
an integral and systematic way. The ENBCS aims to reduce gender gaps by ensuring the participation of 
women i n all the spheres of decision making related to forests, considering the differentiated roles related 
to forests and dependenc e on forest resources between men and  women; and by aiming to ensure equal 
access to services like forest related education, credi t and also to land.  
The implementation of the ENBCS will also contribute to addressing gender gaps in the environmental and 
rural sectors more widely, and contrib utes to meeting the objectives outlined in the National Gender and 
Climate Change Strategy (ENGCC, in Spanish).  T his Strategy , which was published in 2017,  constitutes 
an efficient and effective instrument for the active incorporation of the gender perspe ctive within climate 
change initiatives, so that men and women can live with dignity and use natural resources in a sustainable 
way. Its key pillars are:  
(1) institutional capacity building;
(2) adequate financing;
(3) education, communication and citize n participation;
(4) knowledge management and technology.
The guidelines established in the ENBCS are linked to  the principles established in the ENGCC . 
The ENGCC recognizes existing gender gaps in the country, and links a gender approach with elements 
of the legal framework related to climate change, focusing on Paraguay’s National Policy on Climate 
Change.  The ENGCC constitute s a tool to incorporate the gender approach in the planning process of 
policies and actions to t ackle climate change, by promot ing equal opportunities and engagement of women 
and men in the achievement of sustainable development targets. The Strategy establishes a national

 approach that applies also for REDD+ policies and action s. It determines cross cutting elements  (rights,

define the lines of actio n related to REDD+, such as the  promotion of research projects or enhancing the

c. Gender considerations for th e project “Paraguay REDD+ RBP for results period 
2015 -2017”  
 
Gender sensitive and gender responsive approaches have been incorporated in the modalities of the 
project. Thus, gender considerations have guided the definition of  activities planned for the use  of proceeds, 
and will be key for the implementation of the activities at the community level. The activities  foreseen under 
the project have the potential to support greater equality and empowerment of women in relation to control 
over and access to fores ts, land and natural resources, in particular within the following processes:  
 
• Land -use planning , forest manag ement planning and management planning for protected areas ; 
• Land titling ; 
• Support to sustainable production models , including through access to tr aining, credit and other 
services ; 
• Payment for Environmental Services ; and  
• Control and enforcement of forest and environmental legislation . 
At the same time,  the project will ensure equal opportunities for women and men in employment created 
by the program me, including as part of  the Programme Management Unit  (see Section 4) , in advisory 
services and consultancy, extension , and management of the Climate Change Fund  
In addition, the specific circumstances, gender roles and gaps will be considered at the loca l level before 
and during the implementation of activities, particularly in indigenous peoples’ communities and small 
farmers ( campesino ) communities and evaluated as  part of the monitoring and evaluation process.  
As mentioned previously, the  ENBCS, which  will be supported under Component 1 of the project, aims to 
i) ensure  the participation of women in all spheres of decision making related to forests, considering the 
differentiated roles related to forests and dependency over forest resources between men  and women; and 
ii) ensure  equal access to services like forest relate d education, credit and also to land. Thus, the pr oject 
will contribute to improving  the participation of women and their access to services and resources.  
The gender approach will also  be integrated into the procedures for operation, responsibilities, 
accountability, structuring and the criteria for allocation of resources to the National Climate Change Fund  
(NCCF)  (Component 2  of the project , see Annex 8 to the proposal for more detail ) in several ways. The 
project will ensure equal opportunities for wom en and men in employment created by the programme, 
including as part of the Programme Management Unit, in advisory services and consultancy, extension, 
and management of the Climate Chan ge Fund. Altogether, the project will generate equal opportunities for  
women and men, seeking to reduce existing gaps in the country within the scope of action of the project.

d. Stakeholder consultation and participation  
 
As mentioned above, t he ENBC S has been constructed c ollectively, with a gender approach and the 
promotion of participation of key stakeholders, including women’s groups and civil society. These key actors 
were identified and involved in the REDD+ work following the guidelines of the stakeholder identification 
and mapping . The process of development, socialization and validation of the ENBCS, and th e policies and 
measures it establishes, was carried out with representatives of the public sector, private sector, civil 
society, academia, indigenous peoples, small farmer ( campesino ) organizations, and financial entities, with 
a cross -cutting gender approach and continued engag ement and participation of women’s and civil society 
organizations.  This participation of key actors has been formalized through the REDD+ Working Group, 
which was created and formed within the framework of the National Commission on Climate Change, with 
the aim of becoming a space for discussion and technical consultations on issues related to REDD+.   
The ENBCS is the result of an extensive participatory proc ess with key stakeholders, which started under 
the UN -REDD National Programme from 2011 -2016, and  continued under the FCPF “Forests for 
Sustainable Growth” programme. The Strategy was shared and validated through a process which involved 
306 people , of whi ch 43% were women and 57% were men.   
This project for RBP for Paraguay took as a starting point the diverse and broad consultations conducted 
during the development of the ENBCS; the documentation of this stakeholder engagement process is found 
in the fol lowing link.  In addition, women participated in the meetings that informed the desi gn of the project 
and, also, civil society organizations that work in women issues were engaged  during the meetings 
conducted to develop and review the proposal, including with  representatives of indigenous groups ; a 
special session of the REDD+ Working Group ; and a  meeting  with members of the REDD+ Working Group 
and other stakeholders. Other complementary b ilateral meetings were conducted with the Secretariat of 
Planning for Economic and Social Development (STP, the GCF Nationally Designated Authority), National 
Institute for Rural and Land Development (INDERT), and National Forestry Institute (INFONA).  
Additional information on the consultations conducted for developing the project for the use of RBPs is 
provided in the updated ESMF , regarding the stakeholder con sultations on the use of proceeds (see section 
2.1 of the ESMF document). The specific concerns a nd issued raised are elaborated in the ESMF 
document. Gender is one of the elements considered, and included  with other concerns and inputs 
received. The activ ities included in  the Gender Action P lan were guided by the inputs received during the
